**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Global Economics** Risk-Reward View 

## **Investor Debates** 

- What impact from oil and Japan on growth? 

- Inflation: how strong are the pressures in EM and how will central banks react? 

- Euro Area sovereign debt crisis: Now that Portugal goes to the EFSF, will Spain be able to continue to differentiate itself? 

## **Base Case / Thesis 2011 Global GDP: 4.2%** 

**Constructive on global growth** despite Japan quake and elevated oil prices. US, Japan to rebound in 2H11, Europe to decelerate 

**Global rebalancing remains our key longer-term theme** , boding well for capex and making the expansion more sustainable 

**Global inflation risks still on the up** as higher oil prices still feeding through and monetary policies are superexpansionary. 

**Monetary policy normalization** now starting at the ECB, more to come. Fed to end Q2 in June, then gradually prepare markets for a 1Q 2012  first hike. Bank of England to start in August. 

## **Key Indicators** 

## **What’s in the price?** 

- Risk markets have shrugged off both shocks and don’t seem to worry. 

- ECB tightening fully priced in as is a first Fed hike in 1Q 2012 

- Peripheral bond markets are clearly differentiating between G-I-P and Spain 

##  **BOAST Case** 

**2011 Global GDP: 4.8%** 

Benign Oil And Soaring Trade:  Oil prices easing back to US$ 90 and positive global trade momentum could lift global growth to around 5% and alleviate inflation concerns, thus allowing central banks to tighten more slowly. 

 **TOAST Case 2011 Global GDP: 3.2%** 

Tough Oil And Soggy Trade:  Rising tensions in MENA could push oil prices higher and global supply chain disruptions due to the Japan tragedy could hit global trade more significantly. This would lead to a serious bout of stagflation, posing a dilemma for central banks. Some, like the ECB, would tighten policy more aggressively to fend off second round effects from higher headline inflation, thus aggravating the downturn. 

## **Global GDP: Base, TOAST, BOAST** 

## **Signposts** 

## **During 1H 2011** 

China: One further policy rate hike from PBoC but overall policy stance to support growth 

## **End-2Q 2011** 

Brazil: End of hikes from the BCB; no more hikes until at least 4Q12 

## **May 2011** 

Conditions on Portugal’s bailout expected to be confirmed 

## **June 2011** 

US: Fed asset purchase programme (QE2) to end. 

## **August 2011** 

UK: Bank of England first hike

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Global Equity Derivatives** Risk-Reward View 

## **Investor Debates** 

- Fed policy and the end of QE2 

- Will GDP growth re-accelerate? 

- EU sovereign/banking concerns spreading 

- EM inflation and potential trade or monetary tightening 

- Global monetary policy divergence and currency impacts 

- Impact of higher commodity prices on markets 

## **Base Case / Thesis** 

We believe a sustainable, if bumpy, global recovery will keep volatility low.  Given growth and policy risks, DM skew and term structure will likely remain steep.  Correlation will continue to fall as themes become more localized.  While volatility is similar around the globe, we see significant differences in skew pricing today, reflecting divergent risks. 

With volatility below 15% we think now is a good entry point for long options trades. In the US we favor leaning against longer-term skew for protection, and like near-term upside via calls in the S&P 500.  For short volatility positions we like selling near-term variance in US small caps (in the low 20% for 3m expiries and in the low 30% for 6m expiries) as a carry trade. 

## **What’s in the price?** 

- Although DM markets have rallied and skew and vol have fallen, tail risk hedging remains an important theme 

- The EM picture remains brighter, although inflation is a concern 

- High energy prices coupled with powerful upside risks may provide a headwind to growth 

##  **Bull Case** 

## **VIX in the low teens** 

A continuing straight line recovery strong enough to grow out of financial and sovereign problems, with moderate inflation and a natural end to QE2, would drive volatility towards, but not all the way to, cyclical lows. 

##  **Bear Case VIX in the low 40s** 

A double dip in developed markets, driven by a weak recovery, over-tightening in EM and Europe, European sovereign stress spreading more broadly, or political unrest in the Middle East could drive volatility to cyclical highs (excluding the depths of the financial crisis).  Contagion to core DM sovereigns would drive a more extreme volatility tail. 

## **US Trades** 

Protection: Buy Sep11 S&P OTM PS Upside: Buy 3m OTM S&P calls; Buy 3m bullish RR or butterfly spreads in Russell Yield: Sell Jun11 Russell variance, buy 2012/13 S&P dividends 

## **European Trades** 

Protection: Buy 6m SX5E OTM puts and 6m DAX put spreads 

Yield: Buy 2012/13 SX5E dividends 

## **Asia and EM Trades** 

Protection: Buy 6m OTM Nifty PS; Buy longdated OTM puts on Kospi and AS200; Buy 6, HSCEI puts KI on higher oil 

Upside: Buy calls on MSCI Brazil or Bovespa and MSCI Korea or Kospi; Buy 3m OTM calls on SMISCI; Buy Oct11 TWSE calls KI on TWD; Buy/sell Oct11 TWSE calls / KI puts

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Global Credit Derivatives** Risk-Reward View 

## **Investor Debates** 

- How will the global cyclical recovery fair against fiscal austerity and political risk headwinds in some regions? 

- Will strong fundamentals help corporates overcome macro fears brought on by MENA unrest and global uncertainty? 

- How to isolate credit risk from rates risk, given volatility of rates and rise of inflation concerns in recent months? 

## **Base Case / Thesis** 

Corporate credit continues to be resilient following the events in MENA, Japan, and ongoing headlines in European sovereigns and US municipals. Defaults hit cycle lows, especially in the US, risk premiums decline favoring junior tranche risk. Investors seeking longer-dated tail hedges will continue to prefer buying protection on senior/super-senior tranches. 

Credit options volatility fluctuates but with a tendency to drift lower reflecting the large gap to realized volatility. Today’s steep options skew should moderate but demand for tail hedges will keep it from collapsing completely. Continued differentiation will facilitate interest in smaller basket products. 

## **What’s in the price?** 

- Realized volatility is back to 2007 levels but skew remains steep, as demand for deep OTM puts/payers has not yet abated. 

- Credit curves have steepened on the back of strong corporate liquidity; extending out on the curve is attractive. 

 **Bull Case Defaults, volatility gap lower** 

Macro issues ring-fenced leads to a further decline in volatility. Increased differentiation and rising idiosyncratic risk, along with continued global cyclical recovery helps defaults to remain muted but increases shareholder friendly action, causing tranche correlation to drop. Continued credit improvement and subsequent spread tightening results in a reach for yield and further structured flows. Recovery brings about a sharp rise in rates and further inflation concerns. 

 **Bear Case Correlation and volatility spike** 

Volatility in European sovereigns in the near-term and growth headwinds due to fiscal austerity in DM, causes a spike in volatility, and senior tranche spreads and correlation widen as well. Defaults more likely, particularly in Europe. 

## **Trade Ideas** 

## **TRANCHES:** 

**Long HY15 equity risk:** hedge with junior mezzanine risk in 3yr, or IG9 and HY9 first loss tranches at the 5yr point, to protect against jump-to-default risk and falling correlation. 

**IG9 3-7% 5s7s flattener:** Take advantage of current curve shapes and attractive rolldown. 

**Long 5-year HY15 15-25%, IG15 3- 7%:** We favor at-the-money tranches to play a global cyclical recovery. 

**Short IG and HY senior and super senior tranches:** Senior tranche risk remains our favorite place to implement tail risk hedges. 

## **OPTIONS:** 

**Buy receivers in IG to September** to position for steep skew and continued fundamental improvement in the US. 

## **Correlation and Volatility Decline**

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Cross Asset: Diverging Derivatives Relationships Create Opportunities** 

**Divergence as a baseline:** The “market of one” is behind us as we see an important divergence in volatility and skew across the asset classes. While the correlations between volatility in different asset classes has increased somewhat due to events in the Middle East and Japan, we see this as short-lived.  This theme is applicable across regions as divergent policy drives differentiation within developed markets, while commodity inflation may drive differentiation within EM. 

**More Macro, More Vol:** Volatility in the more “macro” asset classes of interest rates and FX look moderately high versus equities and commodities (the sharp rise in oil volatility has been offset by the fall in gold volatility).  We think this speaks to the strong economic growth and corporate productivity balance sheet story, while the risks are largely policy driven.  This is an important difference versus pre-crisis, when equities was the most volatile asset class.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Cross Asset: Equity Volatility Falls as Risk is Pushed Out the Term Structure** 

**Equity vol falling, skew is diverging:** Equities stand to benefit the most from growth and supportive policy.  Strong rallies in cash markets since 3Q10 pushed global equity volatility to near post-crisis lows, with DM benefiting more than EM as the vol premium between them has increased.  However protection buying remains a prominent theme as downside US skew remains steep, while European skew has fallen on the back of renewed efforts for a permanent crisis resolution mechanism.  We think this relationship is overdone. 

**Credit is a similar story:** The uncertainty caused by the end of QE2 and the timing of a potential Fed rate hike has left downside skew steep, rolldown high, and implied volatility low.  With spreads relatively low this may be a good entry point for upside plays. 

**US Rates:** QE2 has had a depressive impact on rates volatility as inflation seems to be driving expectations more so than robust growth. Near-term implied volatility on longer rates (6m10y) has fallen, but longer expiry volatility has not, providing a good entry to sell variance. 

**FX volatility curves are steep:** FX volatility globally has risen to historically normal ranges on the back of interest rate rises outside US. This makes selling volatility less attractive and carry trades scarce.

**Equities:** KOSPI, IBOV, and the DAX screen as having the cheapest adjusted costs for OTM puts.  Nifty, IBOV, and KOSPI have the best risk/reward to crisis lows.  US indices and DAX have the least basis risk / slippage. 

**Credit and Equity Volatility:** CDX IG options look the cheapest.  We still like super senior protection, as we think the max loss cost is unlikely to be realized. 

**Commodities:** Relationships to equities during downturns are more variable, limiting hedge effectiveness. 

**FX and Rates:** In-line with our views on hedging a China hard landing, TWD puts screen the cheapest. 

(1) Puts are roughly 0.5 standard deviations OTM.  For non-options, cost is the worst-case loss.  (2) Scaling factor is the average of the return ratios in the scenarios listed, recent, and longrun betas.  Periods where correlations to MSCI World were low were dropped.  (3) Average of historical weekly correlations, and correlations between selloffs with the Financial Crisis and Tech Bubble periods. (4) A ranking of basis risk between 0 and 1: using our scaling factors, the average slippage during downturns vs. MSCI World. (5) Credit options are 6m expiries. All 10 pricing indicative.  Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Morgan Stanley Quantitative and Derivative Strategies, Bloomberg

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Global Equities: Cyclical Shift in Volatility** 

**Volatility tells one story:** Cyclical strength has pushed front-end volatility to cycle low ranges across most major markets (Japan is an exception due to earthquake and India is an exception due to inflation and policy-makers desire to sacrifice growth to limit inflation). We believe that front-end volatility should stay low as long as we have evidence that the global cycle is sustainable. 

**While skew tells another:** We see skew diverging across equity markets. Despite the US growth story being stronger than Europe, skews are steeper in the S&P 500, which we attribute to options liquidity and the stronger US hedging culture. This skew difference stands in contrast to the previous cycle. Skews are generally flat in EM with the exception of India given the active fight against inflation. 

**Term structure:** The volatility curve globally remains steep reflecting both long-term growth/inflation uncertainty and weak supply/demand dynamics of longer-dated volatility given changes in the capital and collateral treatment of risk premium monetization trades. 

**US summary:** We remain bullish near-term on recovery and policy (Fed to remain on hold), and expect volatility to stay low and the term structure steep.  We are less bullish over the medium-term as stimulus fades, and advocate 6m put spreads as protection. 

**European summary:** While remaining overweight on Equities, we like owning protection given more growth risks and tail scenarios owing to financials and sovereigns. Flatter skew gives us an opportunity. 

**Asia and EM summary:** We remain cautious APXJ EM equities as earnings risk is increasing and inflation is not turning yet and high oil prices remain a key constraint.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Global Equities: EM vs. DM – From Divide to Differentiation** 

## **The relationship between EM and DM has undergone a fundamental shift as the vol difference is narrowing** 

**Risk factors are becoming more balanced:** With both macro (inflation) and idiosyncratic risk factors rising in EM, we have seen a reversal of fund flows from EM to DM.  However our economics team forecasts EM inflation to gradually fall while DM inflation picks up.  Timing any reversal is key. 

## **With vols nearly flat between EM and DM, look inside each** 

**bloc:** The relative vol story between EM and DM is less clear today than it was in 2010, when EM vol had cheapened considerably versus DM.  We now favor looking within each bloc for ideas. 

## **EM DM vol difference is near zero** 

**==> picture [337 x 160] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
100%<br>80% EM-DM Premium<br>EM Avg Imp vol<br>60%<br>DM Avg Imp vol<br>40%<br>20%<br>0%<br>-20%<br>Apr-08 Oct-08 Apr-09 Oct-09 Apr-10 Oct-10 Apr-11<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


## **Differentiation driven by policy divergence within blocs will emerge** 

**In DM, rates differential is key…** In DM, sovereign concerns and the prospect of higher rates raise risks in Europe, while a burgeoning recovery in the US and the Fed on hold augurs a bumpy but potentially sustained recovery. 

**…while in EM commodity prices are the focus:** EM has seen divergent performance given varying sensitivity to oil prices and inflation, with Russia and Nifty showing big differences in returns. 

## **Correlations between cash and vol fluctuate, but are on a downward path we think**

13 

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Global Equities: EM vs. DM – Trade Ideas** 

## **With vol and skew relatively like, we like upside plays in the US, IBOV, and KOSPI** 

**Play for a continued US recovery:** We like 3m 105% OTM calls on the S&P. 

**We are cautiously bullish:** An appreciating currency enhances domestic demand at the expense of domestic producers, stoking inflation.  However, food inflation seems to be in check and strong demand may outweigh falling output keeping GDP growth healthy. Within EM, we favor 3-month 110% calls in Brazil on the Bovespa, owing to underperformance, a modestly constructive fundamental story, and a commodity producer status. 

**Korea will benefit from the Japanese recovery:** Korea has the industry mix, specifically Autos and Steel, to substitute for the drop in Japanese production. We also like 3-month 105% calls on the KOSPI given low relative volatility and steep skew. 

## **Hedge in Europe and India** 

**Buy protection in EuroStoxx, DAX:** Rising inflationin Europe, tighter monetary policy, and peaking lead indicators are likely to weigh on the equity market, even though vol and skew continue to decline. We think the market may be complacent about downside risks and favor hedging with 6m SX5E 85% puts and 6m 95/85% put spreads on DAX. 

**Bearish on India:** Indian volatility (NIFTY) has reset higher as demand for put buying has increased. Earning and inflation headwinds, risks of policy missteps, and steep skew support our viewpoint. We prefer 6m OTM put spread on NIFTY. 

## **Europe has underperformed the US**

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Skew Flattens Globally, Although DM Skew Remains Somewhat Bid** 

## **In US, upside skew flattens, while downside still steep** 

**US upside skew is falling, while near-term downside skew remains steep:** Despite market healing downside skew remains high (although it has sold off a bit).  Upside skew has flattened with rallies and steepened with falls.  While it is rapidly falling as the market rallies, it remains above levels seen when volatility was this low in 2004-6.  S&P 500 skew for 9m-plus maturities remains rich versus history and EM skew. 

**We favor spreads on the downside; calls on the upside:** We like protecting through 3Q11 over the end of stimulus with put spreads.  With upside skew steep we still prefer outright call options instead of call spreads even though upside sentiment is increasing.  Max loss on both is the premium paid.

## **European skew falls** 

**EMU Summit was a catalyst for skew flattening:** Progress on a permanent crisis resolution mechanism drove skew in Europe lower, and the DM-EM skew premium back to historical norms. We think the move in Europe is overdone.

**Skews in Asia again flattening after recent spike:** Skews across Asia have flattened as markets adjust to Japan earthquake and MENA turmoil recedes from the headlines. NKY continues to have the steepest skew in Asia while NIFTY skew has flattened the most and now trades below Australia in APxJ.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **European Tail Risks Underpriced – Risks Greater in Europe than US** 

## **European downside risks gathering** 

**Rising interest rates, inflations, and currency (EUR) are risks to growth:** In response to rising energy prices, the ECB is forecasted to raise its benchmark rate another 75 bps by yearend.  Our EU Econ team thinks higher rates may curtail both domestic demand and exports, by buoying the EUR.  Higher rates may also exacerbate Portugal’s economic outlook as most homeowners there own variable rate mortgages. 

**Solvency issue remains:** Uncertainty remains on the debt restructuring mechanism for economies approaching insolvency, which may include a principal write-down for private investors and austerity measures.  Furthermore the ESM may not be endowed with its full funding capabilities for years after the EFSF expires (2013) leaving a potential gap in liquidity provision. 

**European equities may feel the pain:** With European leading economic indicators peaking, margin pressures, and inflation impact a difficult period may be approaching.  Our Equity Strategy team favor Defensives over Cyclicals. 

## **While we are cautiously optimistic in the US** 

**2Q11 is critical:** With 1Q growth curtailed bad weather, 2Q growth and unemployment figures will be crucial for gauging if the recovery has legs. 

**Fed on hold:** We expect the Fed to remain on hold as QE2 winds down as growth, rather than inflation, is driving future expectations.  This should reduce US tail risk versus Europe.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Government Intervention Reduces Short-term Vol and Skew… for a While** 

## **Europe making progress** 

## **Financials and iTraxx credit spreads converging** 

## **Authorities have been proactive, and markets are** 

**differentiating:** After the May 2010 Greek crisis, the ECB created the EFSF to provide liquidity to troubled economies and prevent future defaults.  While there has been some progress on a permanent crisis resolution mechanism (ESM), which would address both liquidity and solvency concerns, a final agreement has not yet been forged.  Credit markets, in particular, have anticipated the EFSF applications for Ireland and Portugal, as sovereign CDS term structures have decoupled. 

**Credit spreads for Financials are falling:** As European Financials improve balance sheets and reduce leverage in anticipation of the ECB stress tests, Financials credit spreads are converging to iTraxx.  Talks of an ECB funded SPV to acquire troubled bank assets would be another positive step.  Additionally, the IMF-EU rescue package for Ireland should satisfy the nationalized Irish banks capital needs. 

## **Government intervention has assuaged markets before** 

**After interventions, both volatility and skew typically fall:** Options markets priced a more a sanguine outcome on the back of QE1, QE2, the Q1 Irish recapitalization, and the recent EMU Summit.  However intervention pushes risk out the term structure as longer-term skew and vol remain high. 

**Moves not always permanent though:** As we are seeing with the end of QE2, skew can rise as the market prices a return of tail risk.

**Downside skew is cheap in Europe compared to US:** In Europe, downside skew has fallen with vol despite higher European downside risks. ## **Cross Asset Correlations Have Fallen, But May Rise Again** 

## **Rates, Oil, and FX correlation to Equities has fallen, but may reverse** 

**Equities and oil correlation moving back up after falling:** After correlation fell severely at the start of the year, equities and oil are advancing together again. Over the long-term low spare capacity, strong EM-led demand, and economic growth could drive upside in both assets.  Conditional options are an attractive way to hedge against the bear case of an oil-spike provoked equity correction, although current entry points are less attractive due to the recent decoupling. 

**Correlation between rates and equities has fallen off:** Rallying equity markets and falling 10y rates have caused realized correlation between the two to stay low.  While rates normally should rise as equities advance on the back of a recovery, US specific inflation or less-than-stellar growth could drive negative correlation. 

**Equities and EUR correlation continues to trend down:** As renewed sovereign and banking fears effected European markets and the US recovery takes hold, the EUR had sold off against the USD.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Connecting Credit and Equity Baskets: Sector and Single-Name Differentiation** 

## **Relevant themes emerging just below the macro level** 

**Themes such as inflation, central bank policy, and sovereign and financial issues have become more localized.** At the micro level, we are actually seeing more individualized pockets of performance as well. Investors can play these themes using both credit (FTDs and 2-LTDs) and equity (worst-of-puts and worst-ofcalls) that take advantage of the how each theme will impact a group of names – either similarly or idiosyncratically. 

## **LBOs, inflation-driven margin pressure, and Energy** 

**For potential LBO names, buy FTD protection and sell 9m ATM worst-of-puts:** Often the price action in credit can exceed that of equity following an LBO, as credit risk increases while equity prices can rise.  We like buying protection in credit (a short correlation view) funded by selling a worst-of put in equities. 

**In Energy play for correlated upside by selling worst-of-puts and buying FTD protection to hedge:** In Energy, correlations have fallen as certain firms are more levered to input prices so a long correlation view here makes sense.  We like worst-of calls as well. 

## **Inflation can drive single-name underperformance in the** 

**Staples sector; buy worst-of-puts:** Higher energy and food prices may impact Staples single-names disproportionately.  Since companies pricing and sourcing mechanisms are opaque, a short correlation view makes sense.  We like buying worst-of-puts and selling FTD protection to hedge.

## **Derivatives Across the Capital Structure: Two Tales About Two Tails** 

## **Credit and equity both have comparable large tail hedging instruments** 

**Credit and equity tail instruments:** The markets for super senior protection in credit and long-dated variance in equities both represent extreme tail and systemic risks. Both are less driven by fundamental risks, and more by liquidity, risk/return profiles and market technicals.  Since the financial crisis dynamics have shifted from favoring selling these risk premiums to buying them. 

**Convexity and correlation plays:** Over time, we believe that buying both super senior protection and long-dated variance are good large tail hedges given their convexity, embedded correlation, and duration characteristics. 

## **S&P variance has healed less than senior credit** 

**==> picture [336 x 158] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
200 45%<br>IG 15-30<br>180<br>40%<br>160<br>5y Variance<br>140<br>35%<br>120<br>100 30%<br>80 po e "a<br>25%<br>60<br>40<br>20%<br>20<br>0 15%<br>Jan-06 Jan-07 Jan-08 Jan-09 Jan-10 Jan-11<br>IG 15-30 Credit Spread 5y S&P Variance Strike<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


## **Today, we prefer senior credit tranches to long-dated equity variance swaps as hedges** 

**Super senior protection:** We favor buying investment grade five-year 15-30% type super senior protection with a 6.0x payoff if we compare P&L (from today at ~30bp) to 2007 tights with the 2008 wides. We also like high yield five-year 35-100% protection with a 4.3x payoff. The max potential loss is coupon payments over the life of the trade, +/- any upfront pair or received. 

**Wait for better variance entry points:** While five-year S&P 500 variance can be attractive as a tail risk hedge, we would wait for better entry levels (~20% for 5y and low 20’s for 10y) as payoffs are not as attractive. The maximum potential loss is the level of the strike squared.

## **Trading Tails in Credit-Equity: iTraxx Skew and Vol Rich Compared to EuroStoxx** 

## **European credit volatility and skew are dislocated when compared to equities** 

## **Sovereign and banking concerns more priced into iTraxx** 

**Main:** While credit is most exposed to sovereign stress, this has largely been priced-in as Financial spreads have widened. Credit volatility has decoupled from equity. 

**Historically equity catches up quickly to credit losses:** The greater risk from here to European markets is contagion to corporates.  Further sovereign stress may impact credit markets first, but equity markets generally catch up quickly and powerfully – in 2008, equity markets lagged the credit selloff, but still fell 44% through year-end while iTraxx returned -6.4%. 

## **iTraxx and EuroStoxx have decoupled…** 

**==> picture [335 x 161] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
3,200 50<br>Eurostoxx 50 (LH)<br>iTraxx Main (RH - Inverted scale)<br>3,000 75<br>2,800 100<br>2,600 ! 125<br>2,400 150<br>Jan-10 May-10 Sep-10 Jan-11<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


## **Go long relative steepening of equity skew, or outperformance in credit** 

**Buy Sept SX5E puts, sell Sept iTraxx puts:** To position for equity underperformance or steepening skew, we like selling a Sept 140bp (1.8% OTM) iTraxx put and buying a Sept 15% OTM EuroStoxx put.  We recommend scaling the notionals and strikes by a credit:equity ratio of 6.5x (based on historical vols), for a small net credit (as of May 6, 2011). While hedged, given the short credit put the max potential loss is the notional less recovery. 

**Put spreads for a small tail trade:** We also like buying the June 110-140 bps iTraxx payor spread and selling the June 85/95% SX5E put spread for a 2% net credit (as of May 6, 2011) to position for corporate spreads retracing their outperformance versus financials.  The max potential loss is the width of the put spread.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **US Equities: Risk Pricing Over Earnings** 

## **As differentiation takes hold, idiosyncratic events like earnings become more important** 

**Earnings is usually micro:** Earnings seasons are typically more micro events, and are a good time to setup single-name and sector positions in a falling correlation environment. 

**2Q could be macro though:** Debate on the sustainability of growth is rising, and 2Q could be the a key quarter to determine the trajectory of US growth. 

## **How to play earnings** 

**Options market underprices moves since 2008:** Realized moves have generally exceed implied. While event risk may be underpriced, day-to-day risk has been overpriced with the implied / realized spread positive for the past two years, averaging to something closer to fair value.  The last several earnings periods have been particularly mispriced, and the 4Q10 season (Feb and March 2011) saw significant moves in stocks. On a sector level, cyclicals (Disc, Tech, and Fin) move the most with the defensives (Util, Tlcm, Stpls) moving the least. 

**Where is the options market best at forecasting?** Overall, the market is decent, with a 35% 4y correlation between what the market implied and actual stock moves.  We see variation across sectors, with Telecom quite accurate in predicting earnings moves, while Staples, Healthcare, and Energy are less accurate. 

**3 to 1 week before is the time to position:** Most of the excess jump over earnings gets priced in one to three weeks before the report. 

## **Classic stock and volatility moves over earnings** 

**==> picture [333 x 190] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>

## **Trading the VIX – Which Way to Lean?** 

## **VIX has a futures curve and risk profile comparable to interest rates and credit** 

**Similarities between VIX and USD rates curves:** Currently both rates and VIX futures curves are steep, so leaning _with_ them becomes difficult, while leaning _against_ them becomes very attractive.  While declines in the level of rates make rolldown trades less attractive to them today, the steepness of curves in that market is still an important theme. 

**Similarities between VIX and credit:** We find that both VIX and CDX IG skew are high compared to the S&P.  Given the “jump” risk in both, during times of market stress both have more asymmetric return distributions than cash equities.  On the downside, risk premia (on expected losses for credit and realized vol for VIX) spike when markets sense outsized systemic risk, explaining their fat-tailed distributions. 

## **Today, we prefer put calendar spreads and far-OTM calls** 

**Buy calendar put spreads:** With the fall in VIX outright shorts are difficult.  We would look to calendar spreads to reduce the premium outlay for short exposure. 

**Own the tail of the tail with far-OTM calls:** Upside skew is steep (calls expensive), but instead of cheapening via call spreads, which sell away a valuable option, we like moving far OTM (30 to 35 strikes) to hedge a Greek crisis-like scenario. 

## **VIX rises faster during market stress periods**

## **US Equities: Russell Not Realizing** 

## **Small caps are underperforming, but volatility remains rich** 

**Fundamental drivers of small caps:** The Russell has moved from outperforming to underperforming the S&P 500 as a stronger dollar, higher rates and US outperformance (over EM) have begun to reverse. 

**Russell volatility remains rich:** In comparison to large caps, Russell vol has not fallen as much as the recent rally would suggest. A lag in healing makes sense given the liquidity advantages of the S&P. But with short-term volatility selling opportunities limited in the S&P 500 and the Russell impliedrealized gap higher, we like selling small cap vol. 

## **Small caps starting to underperform large caps**

## **Trade Ideas: Buy upside and sell variance** 

**Buy upside with 3m bull risk-reversals and call butterflies:** Given steep upside skew in Russell compared to S&P, we like the 3m 97.5/104% bullish risk-reversal, which has short vol exposure at the start and provides a small net credit (as of May 6th).  We also like the 105/107.5/110% call butterfly which also has a short vol exposure, while capping both upside gains and downside losses.  The risk-reversal max loss is the put strike less the premium, while the butterfly max loss is the premium paid. 

**Sell Jun11 or Dec11 Russell variance:** We like selling Russell variance instead of S&P, at an 6 vol premium as a carry trade.  We like the June expiry due its coincidence with the expected end of QE2, however we are not opposed to longer-term expiries due to increased liquidity. The maximum loss on a short variance swap is potentially unlimited.

## **Recovery stage dynamics** 

## **Tech upside vol cheap versus Industrials** 

**Sector and stock differentiation is key:** Recovery gaining momentum, earnings continuing to grow, coupled with the fall in intra-market correlation make sector and stock themes more important.  Relative value plays can add much value this year. 

**Sector cycles and valuation:** Our US Equity Strategy team believes Technology is a more favorable cyclical sector than Industrials given cheaper valuations and stronger balance sheets. Within defensives, they team favors Healthcare to Consumer Staples.

## **Term Structures Have Re-Steepened** 

## **Curves steepened as front-end volatility fell** 

**In the US and Europe, risk has been pushed out further:** Term structures remain steep for the level of volatility, although that has moderated some as front-end vol has increased since January lows. 

**Curve has re-steepened:** As Middle East tensions subside and positive US economic data continues, a burgeoning US recovery may take hold keeping short-end low.  On a longer term basis, concerns over government debt burdens, inflation expectations, and still high unemployment should keep long-term risk premiums high. 

## **Most of the steepness remains in the front part of the curve:** 

When comparing the 3m-10y S&P term structure today versus other periods when it was upward sloping (greater than 1% 10y-3m spread), the steepness through 6m remains greater today than typically observed.  As volatility has fallen the steepness is migrating back to the front-end of the curve. 

## **Fade the steepness, but shift to Russell** 

**Sell the Jun or Dec 2011 point on Russell curve:** We are sticking with our short near-dated variance call, but we favor Russell over S&P as the spread looks wide.  We had favored this as a rolldown and carry trade versus 3m realized of 19%, and after a spike in late-Feb on the back of MENA turmoil 10-day realized volatility has risen modestly to 17%, but remains near 3y lows. 

## **Term structure steep for the level of vol**

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Intra-market Implied Realized Correlation Gap at Historical Highs** 

## **Realized correlation has trended lower, while implied is starting to follow suit** 

**Realized correlation has trended lower as markets heal:** Realized correlation (within indices) spiked in May 2010 in developed markets to levels seen during the financial crisis, and remained sticky.  During the recent equity rally though there has been divergent stock performance and realized correlation has fallen, although both implied and realized ticked slightly higher on the back of the Japan earthquake fallout. 

**Sector correlation may differentiate:** Factors such as higher energy and food prices and increased M&A activity may disproportionately impact specific sectors.  Therefore differing sector correlations may materialize over the course of 2011. 

**Options pricing in sustained high correlation:** Investors are still concerned with downside market corrections as evidenced by steep skew and term structure.  As a result, both implied volatility and correlation remain bid, driven in part by the cyclical sectors. 

## **Selling correlation is a compelling trade, but must be patient** 

**Implied correlation should be lower:** Markets have been in a high correlation environment for some time now, but active government and central bank policy have allayed macro risks and as a result realized correlation has fallen. Implied is trending lower but more slowly, and the carry between implied and realized is still large globally.  We continue to like this trade from a carry perspective, although declines in implied will likely take time.

## **US Equities: Dividends Still Discounted** 

## **Bullish on dividends in the US** 

## **Shifting demographics, slow growth, should bolster** 

**dividends:** Potentially slower structural growth and an aging population should increase demand for dividends from companies. 

**Financials are the key:** Basel 3 allows room for US banks to increase dividends, and could spur growth above the 4-5% priced into the front part of the curve. 

## **But focus on the front part of the curve** 

**In the US the curve remains steep:** Dividends followed equities as they gained since the mid May lows, and longer-dated technical flows drove the back end of the curve higher.  While long-dated dividends have given back some of this move, long-term growth still looks too high to us. 

**But near-term growth still looks low:** The 2010 payout ratio was the lowest on record (27%, data since 1900) and near-term growth priced by the dividend swap market remains below consensus EPS growth.

## **European Equities: The Bullish Case for SX5E Index Dividends** 

## **SX5E bottom up dividend estimates** 

## **Trade Idea: Buy 2012 and 2013 SX5E dividend futures** 

**SX5E dividends offer attractive risk-adjusted return compared to equity market:** Despite their outperformance relative to SX5E since May 2010, shorter-dated SX5E dividends (2012/2013) offer higher base case upside and lower bear case downside compared to major developed market indices. We believe that the volatility of 2012/2013 SX5E dividends will continue to decline as we get more visibility. However, the same can not be said about European equity market which is likely to face headwinds due to rising inflation, tighter monetary policy and peaking lead indicators. 

**Attractive fundamental upside remains** : From a bottom-up perspective, we see upside of 9.3% and 20% for 2012 and 2013 respectively. With 2011 dividend futures already trading close to its fair value, shorter-dated dividends (2012/2013) will continue to benefit from higher visibility and pull-to-realized effect. We see positive upside for 2012/2013 dividends even if financials cut their dividends by 25% in 2012. 

**Growth:** Unlike S&P, SX5E dividend term-structure remains inverted beyond 2012. Dividend futures are pricing in consensus EPS to be cut by ~7% and 19.5% for 2011 and 2012 respectively. We believe that this is quite pessimistic. Rising inflation, higher cash on balance sheet and double digit earnings growth are likely to drive dividends growth going forward.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **European Equities: Time to Hedge** 

**Equities likely to face headwinds over the next 3-6 months:** Our European strategy team expects challenging period for equities over the next 3-6 months as (a) Leading indicators are likely to peak in near term (b) Monetary policy is likely to be tighter and (c) Rising inflation remains a risk. 

**Equities tend to fall modestly post peak in leading indicators:** A peak in leading indicators is likely to be the most significant macro turning point in 2Q11. Historically, equities have tended to fall modestly post such an event (See “Our five key investment themes and more detail on leading indicators”, Graham Secker, Mar 28 2011). 

**Expect moderate market weakness as interest rates rise:** Our economists believe that the ECB will raise interest rates thrice in 2011 while BoE will raise rates twice through 2H11. The start of a new rate hike cycle traditionally prompts a six-month period of moderate market weakness. 

**Prefer Dec11 DAX 95%- 85% put-spreads for hedging downside risks:** Post a peak in leading economic indicators, cyclical sectors tend to underperform during rising rate environment. Also stronger Eur is likely to weigh on DAX. We prefer put-spreads as DAX’s downside skew is relatively higher compared to other major European indices and the put spread is ~45% cheaper compared to the outright Puts. 

**Prefer Dec11 SX5E 85% puts for large tail risk hedging:** 6M ATM implied volatility and downside skew is near its lows for SX5E. Investors looking for large tail risk hedges should consider SX5E OTM puts

## **Asia Equities: How to protect against tail-risk in China** 

## **How to protect against tail-risk in China** 

## **The cheapest China “hard-landing” hedges** 

China has one of the most compelling medium-term growth stories to tell among global economies and a hard-landing would have major implications for global markets, particularly for the most China-sensitive assets across equities, credit, rates, FX and commodities. Many investors we speak to are concerned about that tail-risk. We see the probability as low, but not low enough to make hedging costs irrelevant.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Cheapest “China hard-landing” hedges: What has changed?** 

The reward / risk for USDTWD has improved from 6.7x to 7.1x since our last update in February, and is the most cost-efficient hedge against our hard-landing scenario. We see improved reward / risk across all FX trades we recommend, driven mostly by broad dollar weakness. 

China and Korea sovereign CDS hedge profiles are improving with a reward / risk ratio of 5.1x and 5.4x respectively. Sovereign CDS is the most liquid way of positioning China credit risk directly in derivatives form. In an extreme scenario it has the best tail-hedge characteristics, but those are only triggered by credit events. We think sovereign risk is contingent on FX risk and given limited FX debt for Korea and China you would need a major depletion of FX reserves to get anywhere close to a realistic CDS trigger probability. 

KOSPI2 puts replace AS51 puts as the best equity hedge. All equity market hedges look at least as compelling today as in February due to higher valuations and lower vols. A 12% rally in KOSPI2 since our last publication and a decrease in 2y implied vol by 1.32% increases its payout from 1.9x to 3.1x. 

AUD and KRW interest rates, on the other hand, have fallen since February and that is reflected in declining reward / risk ratios going forward. Also, forward premiums have declined as the market is pricing in fewer rates hike.

## **How to compare Tail-Hedges: If I need the hedge to pay $100mm ….** 

## **Comparing the risk/reward of China tail-hedges if Morgan Stanley scenario lows are tested:** 

**Scenario:** we standardize our comparison of the reward/risk of hedges by assuming that a hard-landing scenario takes the various markets to Morgan Stanley’s China hard-landing scenarios. The hedges are all meant to protect against the last 2/3[rd ] of that down move. We then compare costs of getting a $100mm payoff in that scenario (“standardized cost”); the lower the more cost-efficient hedge. We also compare the reward/risk for each position (“Reward/Risk”).

## **Where Could We Be Wron ? g** 

We’ve used scenario analysis and cost-comparisons to evaluate which hedges we’d prefer to protect against a China hard-landing scenario. Clearly there are limitations to this approach. 

For instance, the second-order impact on the rest of the world is hard to handicap. We’ve assumed China weakness impacts other Asian economies but we’ve not assumed a global economic double-dip. Most of our analysts “hard landing” scenarios, especially the equity and commodity scenarios, would be far lower if one assumes a global double-dip _and_ a China hard-landing. 

On the other hand, picking a common historical scenario such as the 2008-09 crisis lows may not be relevant either. In the end, the previous downturn was a Western-banking system driven downturn and the impact of a China driven hard-landing could be more severe, especially on these very China-sensitive markets, but may not involve a breakdown of global financial markets as was the case in 2008. In addition, some things have also changed since 2008, and banks, regulators and governments have already taken steps to improve their level of preparedness, so a downturn could also have more muted implications. 

To illustrate, the refunding of the Korean banking system, the FX swap lines put in place since 2008, the strengthened reserves, the better capitalised and more robustly funded banking system all suggests Korea should weather an identical financial shock better next time. On the other hand, Korea is economically more closely linked to China than the US, and the FX swap lines were indeed with China, which argues for a more severe impact, e.g. USD/KRW hit 1960 during the 97-98’ Asian crisis). 

From a practical perspective, the worst scenario would be one where China has hard-landing, but these hedges don’t work as intended. One scenario would be a breakdown of the correlation between China and its proxies Australia/Korea for instance driven by (i) further contagion-limiting measures by those economies/markets, e.g. further de-risking of the banking systems, strengthening FX reserves and improving fiscal balances, or (ii) a strengthening G10 recovery which makes the proxies less dependent on China. Both are essentially positive scenarios, but for a portfolio that needs to hedge China risk specifically it’s a problem. In those scenarios we think the commodity hedges, especially copper, would work better, even if their reward/risk profile is worse. We also think that equity hedges, e.g. HSCEI puts would be more relevant.

## **EM Equities: Upside in Both Equities and FX – USD or Local Indices?** 

## **Expressing directional equity views in options on local** 

**indices or USD-denominated MSCI indices:** We look at how to make that choice across three markets - Brazil, Korea and Taiwan - where we can see constructive views on both equity and FX and argue that options on the USD MSCI index makes most sense for Brazil and Korea while the local TWSE index is most appropriate in Taiwan. 

**Country-specific upside stories:** We are positive on equities in Brazil (halfway through the tightening cycle in 2011 and inflation coming under control), Korea (sector exposure driving a Japan substitution effect) and Taiwan (growing links to China and growth in Tech spending).

**– Brazil: USD Index Vol Low versus Bovespa Buy Calls on MSCI Brazil (USD):** We prefer positioning upside trades on Brazilian equities on the MSCI Brazil (USD) index over the Bovespa, and we like buying 6 month 105% calls on the MSCI Brazil (USD).

## **Asian Equities: Hedging Inflation Risk in Asia** 

## **Inflation Risk is rising in APXJ** 

## **Headline inflation rising in APxJ ex India** 

**Concerns over inflation and the policy response that it might trigger are on the rise in Asia.** As our Asia Pacific Economics team notes upside risks to headline inflation are materializing due to higher food inflation. However, if commodity prices, particularly oil, rise quickly to US$110 – 120 /bbl, upside risks to inflation would increase sharply. 

## **Asian markets are sensitive to sudden increase in oil and** 

**commodity prices.** First, Asia ex-Japan is an oil intensive economy; per unit of GDP it uses 1.6x as much oil as the rest of the world. Second, all countries in the region (ex-Indonesia and Malaysia) are net importers of oil and related products. Third, commodities (and food) are big components of Asian CPI baskets. 

## **The fundamental backdrop for Oil** 

**Supply demand fundamentals point toward higher oil prices:** . Our commodities strategy team remains constructive on oil, and argues that tightening supply and a fall in spare capacity will lead to higher prices with an estimate of $100/bbl by year-end 2011. 

**At a high enough oil price, Asian stocks and oil are negatively correlated.** Normally oil and stocks are positively correlated and related to growth, but Asia is an oil-intensive economy and also a net importer of oil, which explains why the equity/oil correlation often turns negative at very high oil prices.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Asian Equities: India - increased risk through imported inflation** 

## **India is one of the most oil sensitive economies** 

## **India – exposed to Oil prices and inflation** 

**Inflation risk remains key concern:** Indian inflation at 8.3%, though lower than the recent highs above 9%, is still very high compared to other countries and the central banks stated comfort zone of 5 – 5.5%. Given India’s current inflation-growth mix, it has limited tolerance for any more imported inflation. If reconstruction demand in Japan or instability in Middle East causes commodity prices to rise, India will likely be adversely affected. Our country economist has already reduced the 2012 GDP growth estimates twice this year from 8.7% to 7.7%. 

**High Oil Intensity:** Though not very dependent on Oil for its energy needs, India has a very high Oil intensity (Oil consumption per unit of GDP). This makes pricing of crude Oil key to the inflation outlook and ultimately GDP growth. 

**Implied Volatility Skew one of the highest in Asia:** Though Implied Volatility skew has flattened over the past month, it is still one of the highest in Asia. Inflation and earnings headwinds, and risks of policy missteps remain as possible headwinds. 

## **Buy downside protection on NIFTY** 

**We like 3m 97.5/95% or 6m 95/85% put spreads to position for NIFTY downside.**

**Tail composition matters:** Despite the vast difference in portfolios between IG9 and IG15 (28 names), the real driver of difference in valuations are the four tail names (i.e. MBIA, iStar, Radian and Liz Claiborne). Three of these names are part of 30 name tail of HY15 which trades wider than 500 bp. 

**Renewed focus on idiosyncratic risk:** HY 15 correlation has fallen meaningfully since the start of the year, given renewed focus on idiosyncratic risk. IG9 on the other hand has yet to see correlation fall in a meaningful way. 

**Long HY 15 equity versus short IG9 5Y equity:** Given both single name and correlation views, we like this going long HY equity versus IG equity. We prefer 5Y IG9 IG equity as a short as we anticipate that most issues with the tail credits will come to a head in the next 18 months. 

As of May 4, 2011 **Source:** Morgan Stanley Research 

**Relative value trade economics:** HY 15 has five tail names, which are neither in HY9 or IG9. Even though there is a basis between the two entities and a potentially large mismatch in recovery, given default, the short view is being expressed on the entity which the market considers more risky. The investor needs just 20% of the notional of the IG9/HY9 tranches to be notional hedges. Assuming 40% recovery, the investor is hedged on four names in IG 9 defaulting and around 5 in HY9.

**Super senior tranches as hedges:** Super senior tranches in IG and HY look attractive as lock-in-thedrawer long duration tail hedges.

## **Outri ht Trade Ideas: Junior Tranche Lon s g g** 

## **We have preferred being long various forms of junior tranche risk as a way to play the global cyclical recovery for quite some time now, given economic cycle dynamics.** 

**IG15 3-7% long (5yr):** We initiated this trade last September when the IG15 tranches rolled, and we continue to like it on a thematic level today at 12pts upfront and 100bps running. The very clean portfolio, 3% subordination, and (slightly) positive delta adjusted spread make this trade attractively priced in our view. For those investors who are willing to extend duration in a reach for yield, we see value in extending out to 7 years, though we caution that liquidity is thinner at the 7yr point. 

**Long HY15 15-25% (5yr):** Our preferred long in the HY tranche space is a long-dated junior mezzanine tranche, which we initiated a few months back in _Out the Curve; Up the Capital Structure_ , February 7, 2011. In our view, the 3yr point is already priced for a very low default environment, while the 5yr point is not. Thus, we continue to like this tranche as the best way to monetize the relatively better risk premiums at the 5yr point in HY. The downside is, of course, a severe default environment. 

**HY15 3yr 0-15% long vs. 3yr 15-25% short:** We initiated this trade in _CDX HY15 Tranches, on the Follow,_ October 21, 2010, as the high yield maturity wall peaks after these tranches mature. Given this, the near-term expectation for defaults in HY remains low, and we think this pair trade is a good way to monetize this. The key risk here is an uptick in defaults in the near term, or a risk allocation shift into equity. 

**Source:** Morgan Stanley Research

## **A Twist in the Tail** 

**Idiosyncratic risk making a comeback:** Recent weeks have seen a significant divergence in the performance of the average portfolio versus the tail in the European credit universe. This turn in idiosyncratic risk coincides with the iTraxx index roll from S14 to S15 and represents yet another attempt to transition the tranches from the legacy iTraxx S9 index. 

## **Cleaner portfolio offers interesting tranche** 

**opportunities:** We believe the idiosyncratic risks posed by DSG, better visibility of tails risks in Europe and a cleaner S15 portfolio, offers a number of interesting opportunities across the tranche space. 

**Correlation should decline:** After more than three years of a systemic environment, the tranche market seems to recovering from the correlation inertia. The tail credit DSG could be the catalyst that sparks a move lower in iTraxx correlation.

## **Tail Analysis** 

## **Names in S9 tail but not in S15 Tail** 

**Tail risk differs significantly across portfolios.** With six index rolls between the two series, there are a number of HY credits which are part of S9 but not in S15. We have a clear preference for the S15 portfolio from a long-risk perspective. 

**Portfolio quality not reflected in equity pricing.** Given the vast difference in portfolio quality between S9 and S15 there is little difference in equity pricing, especially at the short end. At the long end, S15 has very little correlation premium above S9, even though the latter faces significant idiosyncratic risk from a single tail credit. 

**Own DSG jump protection.** DSG is in the S9 portfolio, but not S15, and thus buying S9 equity protection versus selling S15 could be a cheap way to hedge DSG jump risk.

## **Mezzanine Risk Plays** 

## **Use longer duration, cleaner portfolios for yield generation** 

**Mezz tranches for yield.** We like S15 3-6% and to a lesser extent 6-9% as longs for investors looking for yield, given our expectation for risk premiums to compress amidst a cyclical recovery. The higher correlation levels also mean that most of the risk premium is allocated away from equity tranches. From a valuation standpoint, 3-6% trades at the wider of the yield spectrum for European ABS. 

**Buy protection on iTraxx S9 5yr 3-6%.** From today through June 2012 captures the period in which peripheral corporate default risk is concentrated. Steep curves and low duration make this trade relatively cheap from an overall cost perspective. This trade offers cheap optionality on tail names.

## **Monetize IG Risk Premiums** 

## **Sell iTraxx Main 3-100% protection outright** 

**Macro developments favor IG credit.** Recent capital raises by Italian and German banks in conjunction with the favorable Irish resolution for senior bond-holders have eased concerns for European investors. As transparency and visibility on tail risks improve, IG credit should be a beneficiary, supporting a decline in risk premiums. 

**Sell iTraxx Main 3-100% protection.** Combining the two views of being short correlation and monetizing IG risk premiums, we like selling protection on the 3-100% tranche outright as an ideal mid-cycle trade.

## **Credit Options Market: Realized Volatility Trending Lower** 

## **Current Volatility and Skew Levels Indicate that the Bid for Tail Hedges Still Strong** 

**Credit fundamentals.** The primary catalysts that our fundamental credit strategy team are watching now are related to the US macro story and more specifically are (1) the end of QE2 and associated implications for credit demand and (2) the rates market and potential impact of a Fed hike. While both could be negative for the credit markets, our credit strategists think fears here are overdone. 

**Volatility trending lower, skew steep.** Since the beginning of 2011, volatility has fallen precipitously, as investors continue to look for ways to cheapen spread widening hedges through the use of bearish risk reversals and payer spread collars. Despite falling realized volatility and a more benign credit environment, upside options remain well offered while downside skew remains steep, reflecting a bid to hedge extreme tail scenarios.

## **Trade Idea: Buy CDX IG Receivers** 

## **Buy CDX IG receivers across option expiries** 

**Fundamentals and valuations support buying upside:** On the back of our fundamental credit outlook and option valuations specifically, we think this is the right time to buy upside as either a cheap way to get long the market for underweight investors or even a low downside way to get longer for those who like US credit. 

## **CDX IG16 Receivers (Ref 90bp)** 

|**Strike**|**Expiry**|**Cost**|**Mid Vol**|
|---|---|---|---|
|80<br>70|Jun<br>Sep|5bp<br>6bp|37.8%<br>40.0%|
|80|Sep|14bp|40.0%|
|70<br>80|Dec<br>Dec|9bp<br>19bp|41.4%<br>43.5%|



Source: Morgan Stanley Research 

**Buy implied volatility below 2%:** In our 2011 outlook we 

expressed our preference for buying options when price volatility is less than 2%. 

**Buy receivers outright :** With OTM receiver volatility trading below 2%, we find buying low strike receivers to be an attractive way to position for upside. Specifically, we like slight OTM (strike 70 or 80) receivers across different option maturities. 

**September is the sweet spot :** Even though we like receivers across expiries, September seems like the sweet spot in terms of time to allow for market tightening versus cost. June expiry receivers are a good buy for investors with strong directional conviction in the next 6 weeks or so.

**Liquidity and long duration:** Many investors look for extreme tail hedges which are cost effective and long duration in nature. These hedges also need to have sufficient liquidity for timely monetization during times of stress. 

**Tail hedges in credit and equities:** We look at longer dated variance and super senior protection across equity and credit markets respectively 

**Systemic risk instruments:** These two instruments have had a disparate investor base and are not necessarily correlated. However, longer dated variance is similar to super senior in terms of its risk remote property and representation of extreme tail and systemic risks. 

57 

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Su er Senior Tranches as a Credit Tail Hed e p g** 

## **Super Senior tranches across IG and HY attach significantly above worst case historical loss rates. Why then should one look at buying super senior protection at current levels?** 

Prior to the credit crisis, there was a lot of super senior risk taking by institutions which were setup well to monetize it as a risk premium trade, often in levered form. These strategies performed poorly following contagion from the subprime and ABS market, thus removing a major technical support for super senior risk. 

We do not expect super senior protection selling today as we saw prior to the financial crisis. Super senior spreads could tighten as a function of overall market tightening, but a return to single-digit spreads is unlikely. 

Instead, super senior protection should continue to enjoy favorable technicals as a large tail hedge instrument.

Though super senior and variance trade at a premium to their fundamental value, selling either is challenging, given the negative convexity of the trade in times of stress. 

We compare trade P&L if were spreads were to touch market tights and wides: 

- **Buy 15-30% protection:** In IG we like 1530% protection as the most attractive hedge. 30-100% tranches are appealing due to their low cost and risk return profile, but suffer from a low absolute payoff in a 2008 type scenario 

- **Buy HY 35-100% protection:** In HY we find 35-100% interesting despite the higher carry, given that the P&L to all time wides is very equity-like at >20% 

- **Avoid buying variance as a systemic hedge:** While SPX variance can also be an attractive tail risk hedge, we think valuations are not attractive today at ~28%. We would recommend investors look at 20% or below as an attractive entry point instead.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Stability in Volatility** 

## **Credit realized volatility should at best grind lower** 

**Realized vol back to pre-crisis levels.** Realized credit volatility is back to July 2007 levels in IG, and HY volatility is almost there as well. This is notable as index spread levels have not fully normalized and are still 3-5x wider than they were in 2007. 

## **Realized volatility declines should be modest and** 

**gradual:** Our focus now is on whether realized volatility can go back to the “old normal”; our current view is that tail risks in certain regions and today’s spread levels will not allow realized volatility to decline too much or too fast just yet. 

**Vol carry remains average:** ATM implied volatility across assets is drifting lower along with realized volatility, but the implied-realized gap is still around recent averages. This is perhaps the new normal, as the crisis has altered the demand for hedging and there will always be marginal demand for options, with certain investors waiting to get involved if the implied-realized gap narrows.

## **Basket Trade Ideas: Increasing LBO Activity** 

## **Basket Trades Offer Downside Optionality, Hedge Bullish Equity View** 

**Corporate Fundamentals:** High cash balances and growing deal interest are not new concepts, but stronger markets often improve sentiment, and following lower deal volume in 2010 this year could see an increase in LBO activity.  Our US IG strategy team expects this to materialize mainly in the US, since Europe’s sovereign and banking struggles should keep any meaningful LBOs off the table for now. 

**Playing Credit vs. Equity:** Often the price action in credit can exceed that of equity following an LBO – at least compared to normal day-to-day volatility, and as such, we like buying downside optionality in the form of long FTD protection. We prefer an FTD structure to a more de-levered 2TD or 2-LTD given the fact that LBOs are highly idiosyncratic events, and the small sampling size inherent in a basket structure already poses a risk that this trade may miss the right candidate.

Based on the public information included in our US Credit Strategy team’s LBO screen. 

**Short Risk in FTD :** A standard 5-name FTD with a sum of spreads of around 685bps would price at around 410bps, or 60% sum of spreads. Alternatively we could up the basket size to 7 names, with a sum of spreads of 1,000bps and an FTD price of 525bps, which, while more expensive, increases the likelihood that one of the names would experience an LBO event. 

61 

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Basket Trade Ideas: Margin Compression** 

## **Long Exposure Through FTDs Can Offset Equity Shorts** 

**Corporate Fundamentals:** In light of rising food and energy prices and rising broader inflation (now not confined to EM and a rising DM risk factor), we see risk to corporate margins – particularly given elevated consensus expectations.  Higher prices can act as a regressive tax on middle income earners who reduce consumption in response. 

**Playing Credit vs. Equity:** While large energy consumers and low-end retailers - transport and retailing industries – typically feel the most pain from any increased energy burden, higher commodity prices have a negative effect on names in the Staples sector, as input prices rise and squeeze tight margins. We expect a more modest impact from margin pressure in credit.

**Long Risk in FTD:** We like pairing equity basket shorts with a long (sell protection) FTD position on a 5 name basket in credit  .  On this basket, the sum of spreads is around 550bps, and selling protection on an FTD would generate around 325bps in premium, which should help offset some of the basket put cost, although we would recommend that an investor scale the notional to be an effective hedge.

**M O R G A N  S T A N L E Y  R E S E A R C H Global Derivatives Strategy** _**Differentiation and Divergence**_ **May 6, 2011** 

## **Basket Trade Ideas: Financials** 

## **2-LTD Long Risk as a Correlation Play** 

**Long Risk 2-LTD :** Most flows in the sovereign and financials FTD space tend to be based on the expected high correlation of such baskets and tend to sell FTD protection. However the implied correlation priced into such baskets is at extremely high levels and makes homogenous plays not great value. 

The realistic long correlation trades in this space involve global, less correlated baskets – such as a global financials basket (Asian, European, US banks, with insurer).  Overall selling 2- LTD protection on financials baskets is a good way to position a constructive view and sell high levels of correlation.

## **Appendix: Strategy Risk Factors** 

**Long Puts/Payors:** Overlaid on a long position, the position is protected below the strike at expiration.  The maximum potential loss in isolation is the premium paid. 

**Long Calls/Receivers:** Overlaid on a short position, the position is protected above the strike at expiration.  The maximum potential loss in isolation is the premium paid. 

**Short Puts/Payors:** The maximum loss is the strike less the premium. 

**Short Calls/Payors:** The maximum loss is unlimited. 

**Buy-writing / Overwriting (selling calls over long stock):** At expiry the risk is that the stock rallies through the short call strike, with the stock called away at the strike price, limiting participation in further upside. Buy- and over-writers retain downside exposure to the stock. The maximum potential loss is the stock purchase price less the premium. 

**Put/Payor Spreads:** Overlaid on a long position, the position is protected between the strikes (but not below) at expiration.  The maximum potential loss in isolation is the premium paid. 

**Call/Receiver Spreads:** Overlaid on a stock position, the position is protected between the strikes (but not above) at expiration.  The maximum potential loss in isolation is the premium paid. 

**Ratio Put/Payor Spreads:** Overlaid on a long position, the position is protected between the higher strike and the lower breakeven point (but not above or below) at expiration.  The lower breakeven is the lesser strike less the put ratio range divided by the difference in quantities, plus or minus the premium paid.  Therefore the maximum potential loss for the option position alone is the lower breakeven. 

**Ratio Call/Receiver Spreads:** Overlaid on a short position, the position is protected above the lower strike and the higher breakeven point (but not above or below) at expiration.  The higher breakeven is the higher strike plus the call ratio range divided by the difference in quantities, plus or minus the premium paid. The maximum potential loss for the option position alone is unlimited due to the net short call position at higher levels. 

**Put/Payor Spread Collars:** Overlaid on a long position, the position is protected between the strikes at expiration.  If the asset rallies through the short call strike, investors could be forced to sell the asset and upside will capped.  The maximum loss on the option position alone is unlimited due to the short call. 

**Call/Receiver Spread Collars (Call Spread + Short Put):** Overlaid on a short position, the position is protected between the strikes (but not above) at expiration, while profit is capped below the strike of the put sold.  The maximum potential loss in isolation is the level of the put strike plus/minus the initial premium. 

**Strangles:** The maximum potential loss on a long strangle is the premium paid. The maximum potential loss on a short strangle is unlimited. 

**Barrier or conditional options:** These are options that can only be exercised if the barrier condition is met and they are in the money. We reference both European-style and daily close barriers in this piece. For European-style the barrier (which decides if the underlying option activates or knocks-in) is evaluated only at expiry. On the other hand, the daily close barrier is evaluated at each day’s closing price, and once active it remains so and behaves like a vanilla European option. For knock-in barrier options the maximum potential loss is the premium paid. 

**Risks to Dividend Futures/Swaps:** Investors long dividend futures/swaps participate 1:1 in movements on the underlying dividend levels. If the dividend index rises the position will show a profit and a loss if the index falls below the entry price. Eligible investors can gain long or short synthetic exposure to the return on the basket through a swap. Typically, the party gaining long exposure via swap will be required to make financing payments to the swap provider. Such payments will be based on various factors, including, credit, liquidity, and borrowability at the time of the transaction. With all swaps, this type of basket swap offers customized exposure based on clients' interest in a given investment strategy and weighting profile. Please contact your Morgan Stanley sales representative for more details. The information contained herein has been prepared solely for informational purposes and is not a solicitation of any offer to buy or sell any security or other financial instrument or to participate in any trading strategy. Swaps may not be appropriate for every investor. Please consult with your legal and tax advisors before making any investment decision. 

**Variance/Volatility Swaps:** At expiry, an investor long/short a variance/volatility swap will be paid the difference between the volatility (squared for variance) and the strike price (or vice-versa for short variance positions) that is realized over the term of the contract. If realized volatility is above the strike price, there will be a gain/loss, and if realized volatility is below the strike price, there will be a loss/gain

## **Options Disclosure** 

Options are not for everyone. Before engaging in the purchasing or writing of options, investors should understand the nature and extent of their rights and obligations and be aware of the risks involved, including the risks pertaining to the business and financial condition of the issuer and the underlying stock. A secondary market may not exist for these securities. For customers of Morgan Stanley & Co. Incorporated who are purchasing or writing exchange-traded options, your attention is called to the publication “Characteristics and Risks of Standardized Options;” in particular, the statement entitled “Risks of Option Writers.” That publication, which you should have read and understood prior to investing in options, can be viewed on the Web at the following address: http://www.optionsclearing.com/about/publications/character-risks.jsp. Spreading may also entail substantial commissions, because it involves at least twice the number of contracts as a long or short position and because spreads are almost invariably closed out prior to expiration. Potential investors should be advised that the tax treatment applicable to spread transactions should be carefully reviewed prior to entering into any transaction. Also, it should be pointed out that while the investor who engages in spread transactions may be reducing risk, he is also reducing his profit potential. The risk/ reward ratio, hence, is an important consideration. 

The risk of exercise in a spread position is the same as that in a short position. Certain investors may be able to anticipate exercise and execute a "rollover" transaction. However, should exercise occur, it would clearly mark the end of the spread position and thereby change the risk/reward ratio. Due to early assignments of the short side of the spread, what appears to be a limited risk spread may have more risk than initially perceived.  An investor with a spread position in index options that is assigned an exercise is at risk for any adverse movement in the current level between the time the settlement value is determined on the date when the exercise notice is filed with OCC and the time when such investor sells or exercises the long leg of the spread. Other multiple-option strategies involving cash settled options, including combinations and straddles, present similar risk. 

## Important Information: 

- Examples within are indicative only, please call your local Morgan Stanley Sales representative for current levels. 

- By selling an option, the seller receives a premium from the option purchaser, and the purchase receives the right to exercise the option at the strike price. If the option purchaser elects to exercise the option, the option seller is obligated to deliver/purchase the underlying shares to/from the option buyer at the strike price.  If the option seller does not own the underlying security while maintaining the short option position (naked), the option seller is exposed to unlimited market risk. 

- Spreading may entail substantial commissions, because it involves at least twice the number of contracts as a long or short position and because spreads are almost invariably closed out prior to expiration. Potential investors should carefully review tax treatment applicable to spread transactions prior to entering into any transactions. 

- Multi-legged strategies are only effective if all components of a suggested trade are implemented. 

- Investors in long option strategies are at risk of losing all of their option premiums.  Investors in short option strategies are at risk of unlimited losses. 

- There are special risks associated with uncovered option writing which expose the investor to potentially significant loss.  Therefore, this type of strategy may not be suitable for all customers approved for options transactions.  The potential loss of uncovered call writing is unlimited.  The writer of an uncovered call is in an extremely risky position, and may incur large losses if the value of the underlying instrument increases above the exercise price. 

- As with writing uncovered calls, the risk of writing uncovered put options is substantial.  The writer of an uncovered put option bears a risk of loss if the value of the underlying instrument declines below the exercise price.  Such loss could be substantial if there is a significant decline in the value of the underlying instrument. 

- Uncovered option writing is thus suitable only for the knowledgeable investor who understands the risks, has the financial capacity and willingness to incur potentially substantial losses, and has sufficient liquid assets to meet applicable margin requirements.  In this regard, if the value of the underlying instrument moves against an uncovered writer’s options position, the investor’s broker may request significant additional margin payments.  If an investor does not make such margin payments, the broker may liquidate stock or options positions in the investor’s account, with little or no prior notice in accordance with the investor’s margin agreement. 

- For combination writing, where the investor writes both a put and a call on the same underlying instrument, the potential risk is unlimited. 

- If a secondary market in options were to become unavailable, investors could not engage in closing transactions, and an option writer would remain obligated until expiration or assignment. 

- The writer of an American-style option is subject to being assigned an exercise at any time after he has written the option until the option expires.  By contrast, the writer of a European-style option is subject to exercise assignment only during the exercise period.

## **Analyst Stock Ratings** 

Overweight (O or Over) - The stock's total return is expected to exceed the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months. 

Equal-weight (E or Equal) - The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months. 

Not-Rated (NR) - Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months. 

Underweight (U or Under) - The stock's total return is expected to be below the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months. 

Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months. 

## **Analyst Industry Views** 

Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. 

In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. 

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. 

Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia - relevant MSCI country index.
